# Growth and Development: Interpreting Graphs and Physical Changes

## 1. Physical Changes During Puberty

| Changes in Boys | Changes in Girls |
| :--- | :--- |
| Hair growth under arms and on legs | Hair growth under arms, legs, face, chest |
| Pubic hair starts to grow | Pubic hair starts to grow |
| Acne / pimples in some individuals | Acne / pimples in some individuals |

---

## 2. The Line Graph (Growth Curve Graph)

* **Note:** Make sure that learners are aware that this graph displays an **AVERAGE** height. It does not just show a comparison between one girl and one boy, but rather takes the average height of a large group of girls and a large group of boys. 
* *Discussion Question:* Why is using an average important? *(Answer: There will always be exceptions—older people who are shorter than younger people, and girls who are taller than boys—but using the average helps us see the general trend that occurs in most people.)*

---

## 3. Average Increase in Height
* The average increase in height of boys and girls between birth and $18$ years of age.

---

## 4. Variables in the Growth Graph
* **Independent Variable:** The age of the boys and girls.
* **Dependent Variable:** Average height increase.

> **Note:** Height depends on the age of the boys and girls; therefore, height is the **dependent variable** and age is the **independent variable**. As children grow older, they grow taller.

---

## 5. Units of Measurement
* Height is measured in centimetres ($\text{cm}$).
* Age is measured in years.

> **Note:** The axes of graphs must always have labels. If there is a unit of measurement, it must always be included in brackets after the heading (e.g., Height ($\text{cm}$) vs. Age ($\text{years}$)).

---

## 6. Interpreting Growth Graphs

Interpreting graphs is a crucial skill in Natural Sciences. Follow these steps when analyzing a growth graph:

* **a)** The **blue line** represents the average change in height of boys as they grow older. The **red line** indicates the average change in height of girls as they grow older.
* **b)** Boys and girls are on average the same height up until $6$ years of age.
* **c)** Between age $6$ and $10$ years old, the graph line for boys is on top, indicating that boys are generally taller than girls during this phase.
* **d)** Around age $10$, boys and girls are roughly the same height again (where the two lines cross or **intersect**). At around age $14\frac{1}{2}$, boys and girls are once again the same height.
* **e)** At age $18$, boys are generally taller than girls. The average height of boys at $18$ years is about $187\text{ cm}$, and the average height of girls is about $171\text{ cm}$.

> **Note:** Teach learners how to read values off the graph using rulers. Draw a horizontal line across from $18$ years on the x-axis to the y-axis to find where it intersects, then estimate the height for boys and girls.

---

## 7. Analysis of Growth Spurts

* **a) Sharp Incline:** A sharp incline in the line of the graph indicates a **growth spurt**.
* **b) Constant Growth:** Growth is not always steady and uniform throughout childhood.
* **c) Age of Growth Spurts:** 
  * Boys show a growth spurt between the ages of about $14$ and $16$ years.
  * Girls show a growth spurt between the ages of about $11$ and $13$ years.
* **d) Connection to Puberty:** These growth spurts correspond to the ages when girls and boys go through puberty. Girls go through puberty slightly earlier than boys, meaning their growth spurts take place at different phases.

---

## 8. Practical Activity Guidelines
* **8.** Individual learners to mark this on the graph.
* **9.** Learner-dependent answer.
* **10.** Learner-dependent answer.

![Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr7_Biodiversity.pdf-0056-00.png](images/Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr7_Biodiversity.pdf-0056-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_NaturalScience_Gr7_Biodiversity.pdf-0056-00.png]**
> The graphic is a comparative table featuring photographs of four animals labeled E, F, G, and H, representing a Tortoise, Frog, Crab, and Earthworm respectively. Visible labels include categories for each animal such as "Animal," "Type of skeleton" (specifying Endoskeleton or Exoskeleton), and "Vertebrate or invertebrate." This table conveys the biological classification and structural adaptations of different animal groups, helping students distinguish between internal skeletons (endoskeletons) in vertebrates like reptiles and amphibians, external skeletons (exoskeletons) in arthropods, and hydrostatic support systems.

# Understanding Puberty and Emotional Development

## Discussion and Reflection Activities
* **Ages 25 to 35:** Height generally remains stable during this period.
* **Ages 65 to 85:** Older individuals often experience a slight decrease in height due to the compression or curving of the vertebrae.

## Teacher Guidance and Self-Reflection
Educators are encouraged to coordinate with the Life Orientation teacher regarding topics such as sexual maturity, emotional changes, and self-awareness. 

* **Suggested Activity:** Grant learners 10 minutes of silent self-reflection outside the classroom, followed by a class discussion.

---

## Key Emotional and Psychological Changes During Puberty

### 1. Strong Feelings
* Puberty involves transitioning between intense emotions. When these emotional states last longer than a few minutes, they are referred to as **moods**.
* Rapid shifts can occur (e.g., feeling excited and happy with friends, then feeling sombre, down, or sad without a clear reason).
* This emotional fluctuation can cause anxiety and frustration, potentially leading to conflicts with parents or siblings.
* **Coping Strategy:** Learning to handle conflict constructively and recognizing when to remove oneself from situations prone to unmanageable emotional outbursts.

### 2. Increased Sensitivity
* Young people experiencing puberty may become highly sensitive to the actions and intentions of others, sometimes misreading facial expressions (often perceived as being "over-sensitive").
* As they mature, individuals learn to interpret facial expressions and body language more accurately, helping them understand hidden or figurative messages and respond in a controlled manner.

### 3. Increased Self-Consciousness
* Physical and emotional changes can heighten self-consciousness, leading to increased concern over physical appearance.
* Adolescents may compare themselves to celebrities, friends, and role models. Dissatisfaction with their appearance can impact self-image and **self-esteem**.

### 4. Seeking New Experiences
* Driven by hormones, young people are often inclined to explore unknown paths and seek new experiences.
* This curiosity can occasionally lead to dangerous, risk-taking behavior that poses physical danger or threatens future plans.
* **Goal:** Developing impulse control to make wiser, safer decisions.

### 5. Exploring Sexual Identity
* Adolescents begin forming romantic relationships and going on "dates."
* While some choose to wait until they are older before engaging in sexual relationships, others feel curious about sex.
* **Importance:** Understanding the risks involved and taking proper precautions to protect one's health and future is a primary focus of this curriculum chapter.

## Addressing Myths Surrounding Menstruation

You should go through each of the myths to show that they are in fact all myths — they are NOT true. Read through these explanations and refer to them during your discussion with your class.

### 1. "Women who are menstruating are dirty and unclean."
* **Explanation:** Women are not dirty and unclean during menstruation as menstruation is a perfectly natural process. Some people do believe this for religious reasons and you should be sensitive to their religious beliefs, while still stating that women are not "dirty".

### 2. "During your period you should never have cold food or walk with bare feet. If you get cold your period will be worse."
* **Explanation:** This is not true. The uterus is the organ that controls menstruation and cold feet or food have nothing to do with the uterus.

### 3. "Exercise is bad for you when you menstruate."
* **Explanation:** Menstruation is a normal, natural function of the body and not in any way a disability. Therefore you should continue with life as if nothing out of the ordinary is going on.

### 4. "Don't ever swim when you're having a period!"
* **Explanation:** If using the right protection (tampons) there is no reason why girls should not be able to swim while they are having their periods.

### 5. "Virgins cannot use tampons – they will lose their virginity."
* **Explanation:** Virginity is about whether a woman has had sexual intercourse or not. Long ago, a woman's virginity used to be assessed by whether her hymen was intact, as this ruptures during sex. However, nowadays, there are many times in a girl's life when her hymen can break without having sex, such as by using a tampon or horse-riding, or by doing gymnastics or ballet. This does not mean she has lost her virginity.

# Myths and Facts Regarding Sex, Menstruation, and Pregnancy

## Common Misconceptions Addressed

### 1. "It is unhealthy to have sex at the time of the month when you are menstruating."
* **Fact:** Although some people might find it off-putting, there is no medical reason why sex should be avoided during menstruation. It is not unhealthy or unclean; it is simply the personal preference of the couple.

### 2. "You cannot fall pregnant during your period."
* **Fact:** There is a chance that you can get pregnant if you have sex during your period. Once inside the vagina, sperm can stay alive for several days. This means that even if the last time you had sex was three days ago during your period, you could now be ovulating and therefore could get pregnant.

### 3. "You cannot fall pregnant or make someone pregnant if you have sex in water."
* **Fact:** This is not true. The water does not in any way prevent pregnancy or the spread of STDs (Sexually Transmitted Diseases).

### 4. "Women are always moody and irrational during menstruation."
* **Fact:** Many women (not all) experience different pre-menstrual syndrome (PMS) symptoms, which can include emotional changes. However, this is not always so, and a woman's mood or emotional state should not automatically be attributed to PMS. Teenage boys can be similarly moody and emotional.

### 5. "Drinking and drugs make sex more fun."
* **Fact:** If you are drunk or on drugs, it is hard to make good decisions about sex. Statistics show that $20\%$ of $15\text{- to }17$-year-olds say they have done something sexual while using alcohol or drugs that they might not have done if they were sober. Being under the influence makes you much less likely to practise safe sex, putting you at risk of unwanted pregnancy or contracting an STD. It also makes girls more vulnerable to unwanted approaches.

### 6. "If you have a shower after sex, you will not fall pregnant."
* **Fact:** This is not true. Water will not 'wash off' the sperm, as the sperm have already entered the vagina. Similarly, showering after sex will not prevent pregnancy nor the transmission of STDs.

### 7. "You cannot fall pregnant if it is your first time."
* **Fact:** If you are ovulating, it does not matter if it is the first time you have sex—you can still fall pregnant. If your body has produced an egg during ovulation, sperm can fertilise the egg and result in pregnancy, regardless of whether it is your first time.

### 8. "Everyone is having sex."
* **Fact:** It may seem as though everyone is having sex, but in reality, less than half ($48\%$) of all high school students have ever had sex. People often lie and exaggerate when it comes to sex. Ultimately, what others are doing does not matter; what matters is what is best for you. 
* **Note on Peer Pressure:** Partners may apply pressure by saying things like *"If you love me, you will"*. However, a truly loving and committed partner will wait until you are ready.

---

## Classroom Discussion Points

* **Derogatory Terminology:** At the end of this lesson, discuss derogatory words used with reference to women and men. Brainstorm bad name choices and emphasize the importance of using correct, respectful terminology.
* **Cultural and Religious Perspectives:** If there are different cultural and religious groups in the class, it is an opportune time to discuss religious laws and views on menstruation, as well as what girls experience as part of these groups. Teachers must ensure an open, caring space and avoid degrading or embarrassing any learners based on their family's religious beliefs.

---

# Ways to prevent pregnancy and STDs

Teenagers may be more frightened by a possible pregnancy than by contracting a STD. This is a sad fact, as some STDs are incurable and life-threatening. It is useful to point out to learners in this section that **hormonal contraceptive pills DO NOT protect against STDs** and that a mistake made when they are young may affect the rest of their lives.

You may want to point out to learners that due to the way that STDs are transmitted from person to person, when having sexual intercourse with a partner you are putting yourself at risk of catching disease from them, or ANY of their previous sexual partners, who in turn could have contracted an STD from any of their sexual partners etc.

Emphasise to learners that if someone is forcing you to have sex with them or threatening you in any way to have sex with someone else, you have the right to say no. Our country's constitution protects your right to say no. If this is happening to you, go and speak to someone you trust and ask them to help you solve the problem.

Emphasise to learners that when you decide that you want to have sex with someone, go and speak to a medical professional like the sister at your local clinic or the health care professional that works at the pharmacy. They will help you get the correct contraceptives and protection that you need. Emphasise that it is not only boys who can carry condoms. Girls can carry condoms too. If girls have condoms available, then they can INSIST on using protection.

## ACTIVITY: Write a letter (LB page 76)

This activity is designed to empower the learners to take charge of their own sexual health. This is therefore a very personal activity and should not be for assessment purposes. Learners may choose to keep their letters entirely private.

### Key Concepts and Definitions

#### Flower Parts and Pollination
| Term | Definition / Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| **pollen grains** | Fine powdery substance that contains the male sex cells that are transported to the stigma, and burrow down the style to the ovules where fertilisation takes place. |
| **Filament** | Stalk-like structure of the stamen that holds and supports the anther. |
| **Receptacle** | Top part of the flower stalk to which all the other flower parts attach. |

---

### Plant Adaptations and Seed Dispersal

3. **Bat Pollination Adaptations:**
   * **Colour & Scent:** The flower is brightly coloured and gives off a sweet-smelling scent to attract bats.
   * **Nourishment:** Contains nectar for the bat to drink.
   * **Timing:** The flower is typically open at night to match the nocturnal habits of bats.
   * **Structure:** Shaped similarly to the bat's head so the bat fits easily into the flower to reach the nectar.
   * **Size:** Large enough with nectar at the bottom, requiring the bat to push its whole head in, brushing against the pollen.
   * **Stamens:** Long with feathery ends designed to brush against the bat as it feeds.

4. **Wind Dispersal Adaptations:**
   * **Structure:** The seed features very thin "wings" enabling it to travel on the wind like a helicopter.
   * **Purpose:** Helps the seed move far away from the parent tree to avoid competition for space, water, and resources. 
   * **Mechanism:** Allows the seed to be lifted by drafts of air rather than falling straight down to the ground.

---

### Human Reproduction

5. **Sequence of Fertilisation and Implantation:**
   * **1 or 2:** The egg is released from the ovaries and travels along the oviduct.
   * **2 or 1:** During sexual intercourse, the sperm is propelled from the penis.
   * **3:** The sperm travel from the vagina, through the uterus to the oviduct.
   * **4:** The sperm arrive in the oviduct.
   * **5:** One sperm enters the outer cover of the egg to fertilise it.
   * **6:** The fertilised egg is implanted in the uterine lining.

6. **Key Reproductive Processes:**
   * **Ovulation:** When the ovary releases a ripe egg cell into the fallopian tube.
   * **Menstruation:** When the blood-rich lining of the uterus and unfertilised egg cell are discarded through the vagina.

7. **Site of Implantation:**
   * Implantation takes place in the **blood-rich lining of the uterus**.

8. **Female Reproductive Organs and Their Functions:**

| Reproductive organs | Their function |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Ovaries** | Produces female sex hormones and stores, matures and releases ripe egg cells. |
| **Oviducts** | Transports the ripened egg cell from the ovary to the uterus; fertilisation takes place in the fallopian tubes (oviducts) so sperm swims from the uterus into the fallopian tube (oviduct). |
| **Uterus** | Once a month the lining of the uterus grows blood-rich and thick to allow the fertilised egg to implant in it and to grow an umbilical cord and placenta; uterus also undergoes strong contractions during childbirth. |

---

# Reproductive System and Key Biological Concepts

## 1. Reproductive Organs and Functions

| Organ | Description and Function |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Vagina** | Muscular, elastic tube that can expand to hold the erect penis; sperm swim from the penis in the vagina to the uterus; it also allows the blood-thick lining of the uterus to be expelled once a month during menstruation; during childbirth it is the birth canal through which the baby travels and leaves the mother's body. |
| **Penis** | Can become erect (stiff and hard) to be placed into the vagina; ejaculates sperm in a liquid called semen; urine is also passed via the urethra. |
| **Testes** | Produce the male hormone; produces sperm that travel through different tubes to the penis from where it is ejaculated. |

## 2. Puberty and Reproduction Notes

9. The penis grows longer and slightly wider; it begins to ejaculate preparing for reproduction. The testes mature and start to produce sperm which can fertilise an egg.
10. During puberty, the ovaries are stimulated to start maturing and releasing the eggs on a monthly basis. The fertilisation of a mature egg by a sperm cell is needed for pregnancy to occur.
11. 

## 3. Evaluation of Biological Terms and Definitions

| Term and definition | Evaluation | Improvement |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Puberty:** When you grow up. | Vague and not accurate; you grow up from birth to adulthood. | Puberty is the time during childhood when the sex organs mature and the body undergoes various changes, preparing it for reproduction. |
| **Menstruation:** When a girl bleeds. | Vague, inaccurate – if a girl cuts or hurts herself she may also bleed but not as is meant here. | If there is no fertilisation, the female's blood-rich uterus lining and unfertilised egg are discarded through the vagina once a month. |
| **Fertilisation:** When you put stuff into the garden to make it grow better. | Wrong context – that is called fertilising the garden. | When a male sperm cell fuses with a female egg cell. |
| **Pregnancy:** When the mom's stomach grows, and a baby pops out. | Inaccurate – it is not the stomach that grows, but the baby in the uterus. And a baby does not 'pop out' | The period (approximately 40 weeks) from conception to birth, where the foetus develops in the womb or uterus. |
| **Conception:** When the baby starts to come alive. | Inaccurate – life starts for some at different points | The moment that fertilisation takes place when the male sperm fuses with the female egg cell and forms a new individual. |
